# Gillis van Coninxloo: Study Notes

**Site:** Gillis van Coninxloo II (24 January 1544, Antwerp - buried 4 January 1607, Amsterdam), Flemish landscape painter; the man who took the forest off the edge of the picture and made it the picture -- close-up woodland with the sky shut out, trees on a hugely exaggerated scale, and human figures shrunk to footnotes. He is now numbered II (previously III, after Miedema 2014); the son who painted still lifes is Gillis III (c. 1581-1619/20). Only six signed paintings are secure; the rest of the oeuvre is attribution, circle, follower and copy. His painted forest landscapes all postdate his move to Amsterdam in 1595, and his influence spread less through the paintings than through the large-scale prints engraved after his designs by Nicolaes de Bruyn and Jan van Londerseel.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Hendrick Goudt study: the engraver who lodged in Elsheimer's house in Rome and carried the nocturne north in print -- his 1613 Flight into Egypt is the plate that taught a whole school to paint the night. The wooded landscape itself was made a generation earlier by Coninxloo, whose pupil Segers etched after Goudt's plates -- follow the forest back to its founder, and the night back to the trees it hangs in. The link is the pupil and the print: Coninxloo taught Hercules Segers, Segers made a free copy of Goudt's print of Elsheimer's lost Large Tobias, and Rembrandt reworked Segers's plate. This study is the forest at the head of that chain -- the trees the night hangs in.

One-hundred-and-seventy-fifth doorway check in a row. D1 "Gillis van Coninxloo (24 January 1544 - January 1607; buried 4 January 1607)" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (birth 24 January 1544 holds -- leg 1 F1; burial 4 January 1607 in the Nieuwe Kerk, Amsterdam, is the documented anchor -- leg 1 F14; but "January 1607" as the death month does not -- leg 2 C8: the will was made 7 December 1606 and he died later in December 1606, so he died in 1606 and was buried in January 1607; leg 1 C1 allows death in the first days of 1607 only as the weaker alternative). D2 "the Antwerp painter who invented the close-up forest landscape" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter ("invented" as flat fact does not -- leg 1 C6 and leg 2 C3: Pieter Bruegel the Elder's forest drawings of the 1550s precede him, Jan Brueghel the Elder experimented with dense forest landscapes before Coninxloo's first dated wooded landscape of 1598, and Van Mander himself makes no claim of originality for him; what holds is that Coninxloo elaborated the type and took it to its high point -- he is the systematiser, not the inventor). D3 "taught Hercules Segers" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (Segers was apprenticed to Coninxloo in Amsterdam in 1606, the apprenticeship cut short by Coninxloo's death -- leg 1 F19, leg 3 N17). D4 "the etcher who carried Goudt's night into print and whose plate Rembrandt reworked" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (the etcher is Segers, not Coninxloo: Segers's Tobias and the Angel plate is a free copy of Goudt's print of Elsheimer's lost Large Tobias, and Rembrandt reworked that Segers plate -- leg 3 N64). D5 "Goudt engraved Elsheimer's wooded nights and carried them north in print in 1611" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (he did carry them north in print -- the Flight engraving, 1613, after Elsheimer -- but the year 1611 is the return to Utrecht, not the print: Goudt was back in Utrecht by 1611 and engraved the Flight there in 1613 -- see #553 D5; the hop sentence attaches the print to the wrong year). ## The life

Gillis van Coninxloo was born in Antwerp on 24 January 1544, the son of Gillis van Coninxloo (a painter from Brussels, master in the Antwerp Guild of Saint Luke in 1539) and Adriana van Doornicke, daughter of the painter Jan van Dornicke and widow of the painter Jan van Amstel; her sister married Pieter Coecke van Aelst. His father died when Gillis was about ten months old. His mother made a living selling paintings in Antwerp and in Brabant and Flanders towns, and remarried on 20 December 1545 to the painter Peeter van Else, alias van den Winckele.

He studied first under Pieter Coecke van Aelst the Younger -- son of his uncle Pieter Coecke van Aelst, whom Van Mander calls his cousin -- and after that master's death in 1559 continued with Lenaert Kroes. After his mother's death in 1562 he lived with the painter Gillis Mostaert, working in his workshop in return for food and lodging, and allowed to work for his own account. He travelled in France (Paris, Orleans), planned to continue to Italy, but returned to Antwerp on learning his cousin Pauwels Coecke van Aelst had died, to marry the cousin's widow Maria Robroeck; they married in 1570 and had at least two children: Catalijne (b. c. 1579) and Gillis III (c. 1581-1619/20), who became a still-life painter. He became a member of the Antwerp Guild of Saint Luke in 1570.

He ran a very busy workshop producing large landscape paintings sold internationally, including to the king of Spain. He sided with the Protestant rebels, was active in the defence of Antwerp during the Siege (1584-85), and in January 1585 received a large monetary reward from the outer mayor Philips of Marnix for his role; he left soon after, as Antwerp fell to the Spanish the same year. He first resided in Middelburg, Zeeland, then in 1587 moved to Frankenthal, where he was active until 1595; he then moved to Amsterdam. His presence in Amsterdam is first registered in the Poorterboeken on 22 April 1597.

After his first wife's death in 1603 he remarried in Amsterdam on 23 August 1603 to Geertgen (Geertje) van Eede, or van Eden; the marriage banns record him living on the Turfmarkt and note he had lived in Amsterdam for eight years. Wikipedia gives the second wife as "born 1675" -- impossible, since she married him in 1603; almost certainly a typo for 1575, and not repeated here. He probably lived on the Turfmarkt in Amsterdam (1603-1607), in a house rented from Arent ten Grootenhuys, though the identification of the exact house is probable, not certain.

He died in Amsterdam in December 1606 -- the will was made 7 December 1606 -- and was buried in the Nieuwe Kerk on 4 January 1607. A probate inventory of his possessions survives (Amsterdam City Archives, notary Frederik van Banchem, fols. 68-88, 11 January 1611, dated after the estate auction); it lists many paintings including unfinished ones and much art material, suggesting he ran a shop at home -- "Coninxloos winckel" and an "achter winckel" on the first floor. After his death the auction of his estate gathered almost the entire Amsterdam artists' community; Segers and his father bought works there, as did Pieter Lastman.

His pupils included Pieter Brueghel the Younger, Govert Govertsz van Arnhem, Willem van den Bundel, Gillis van Coninxloo III, Jonas van Merle, Hercules Seghers and Jacques van der Wijen. Karel van Mander wrote in the Schilder-boeck (1604): "I know at this time of no better landscape painter, and I notice that they are following his manner very much in Holland." He exercised a strong influence on Jan Brueghel the Elder, Pieter Schoubroeck, Roelandt Savery, and other Flemish and Dutch landscape painters; in Amsterdam his younger-generation followers included Roelandt Savery and David Vinckboons. During his Frankenthal stay he influenced the Flemish emigre landscape painters now collectively called the Frankenthal School, including Anton Mirou and Pieter Schoubroeck. His influence spread in Holland through his designs for large-scale prints, mainly engraved by Nicolaes de Bruyn and Jan van Londerseel.

Afterlife: in the first half of the 20th century he was still regarded as the "inventor" of the wooded landscape; it is now known the type was developed earlier, in Pieter Bruegel the Elder's drawings of the 1550s, and Van Mander himself makes no claim of originality for him. What he did was take the forest to its high point: beginning in the 1590s he introduced close-up views of forests, almost or entirely shutting out a distant view, and turned the forest from the backdrop of human activity into the subject proper. His painted forest landscapes all postdate his move to Amsterdam in 1595. He owned a copy of Justus Lipsius' De Constantia, indicating an interest in neo-Stoicism. Only Coninxloo, Hans Bol and Jacob Savery were established Flemish landscape painters who continued to paint in Amsterdam after emigrating; most southern landscape painters emigrated young and trained in the north.

## M1. The close-up forest: shutting out the world landscape

What it IS: instead of the panoramic "world landscape" (high viewpoint, three colour-zones receding to a blue distance), Coninxloo fills the whole panel with forest at close range. From the 1590s the trees are the subject, not the frame; the distant view is "almost or entirely shut out", and in the 1598 Forest Landscape the sky survives only as "a few patches between branches". -- Leg 1 F26, F27.

**Takeaway:** enclosure is the subject -- remove the horizon and the forest stops being scenery and becomes a place you are inside.

## M2. Low vantage point with no entry and no exit

What it IS: the viewer looks from a low point past a foreground of "meticulously rendered vegetation, dead branches, and marshy underwood into a substantial woodland" -- and is "provided no access into nor road out of the scene" (Levesque on the KHM Forest Landscape). No path leads in; no road leads out. -- Leg 1, Levesque P4.

**Takeaway:** a landscape with no path is a landscape that refuses the viewer -- the tension of being inside without a way through is the whole feeling.

## M3. Exaggerated tree scale, submerged figures

What it IS: trees are painted "on a hugely exaggerated scale" while human figures are shrunk to near-invisibility -- a single tiny reclining figure in the 1598 Liechtenstein panel; in the KHM panel "no single human figure is portrayed. Only small animals can be found". Figures become staffage: hunters, a shepherd with a staff lit by a patch of light, a small man running away into the woods. -- Leg 1 F27, leg 1 P5.

**Takeaway:** scale is the argument: make the trees enormous and the human a footnote, and the forest reads as a power, not a backdrop.

## M4. Fine green/brown tonal gradation + accentuated light (canopy chiaroscuro)

What it IS: the signature effect is "great intensity and atmospheric quality through its fine shades of brown and green and its accentuated handling of light". Light does not flood the scene; it is rationed -- sun rays piercing cloud and canopy, a patch of light on a reclining man, glints on leaves. Van Mander's catalogue of effects is all weather and light: sun through clouds, dark rain clouds, hail, snow, "all possible variations in green". -- Leg 1 F27, F32.

**Takeaway:** atmosphere is made by withholding light, not by adding it -- a few lit patches inside a dark green-brown mass do more than a bright sky ever could.

## M5. Brownish imprimatura shining through the leaves

What it IS: the panel is prepared with a brownish ground (imprimatura) deliberately left visible, glowing through the thinly painted foliage -- observed directly by Boutsen on the Milotice panel: "the atmospheric forest was rendered in very rich colours with the typical brownish imprimatura shining through the leaves". Leaves are painted thinly enough that the warm ground participates in the colour. -- Leg 1 F31.

**Takeaway:** let the ground do the shadow work -- a warm imprimatura under thin greens gives the forest its damp, breathing depth for free.

## M6. Botanical specificity: bark, roots, marsh, iris

What it IS: trees are rendered with "intricate and accurate botanical detail" -- "firmly grounded trees with prominent roots, thick full girthed trunks, broad leafy branches, ample canopies of foliage"; Van Mander's Den Grondt singles out "the distinctive texture of the trunks, pale thin birches and furrowed oak bark". The foreground is marshy underwood: broken trees, grasses, irises, brambles, mosses. -- Leg 1, Levesque P4.

**Takeaway:** detail is densest at the feet and thins with distance -- the forest is built as a gradient from specimen to atmosphere.

## M7. The Frankenthal counterbalance (close foreground vs. distant panorama)

What it IS: the Frankenthal-school compositional pattern (per the Warsaw Bulletin summary): "from one side a closely viewed foreground with small figures, bordered by a lateral group of high trees, is counterbalanced on the other side of the composition by a distant panorama" -- a preconceived pattern built from realistically observed elements subordinated to painterliness. This is the transitional formula between the world landscape and the pure forest interior. -- Leg 1 S7.

**Takeaway:** enclosure needs one opening to be felt -- a single lateral vista makes the forest's density legible by contrast.

## M8. Print dissemination: designs as a motif kit

What it IS: Coninxloo's inventions travelled as engravings (Nicolaes de Bruyn, Jan van Londerseel) rather than as paintings; his pre-Amsterdam output was largely prints. Followers and copyists then assembled landscapes from these printed motifs -- the Met follower drawing combines a castle from one print and cottages from another. The KHM panel alone was copied at least six times. Copies simplify the leaves into repetitive patterns and lose the atmospheric depth -- the motif survives, the handling does not. -- Leg 1 F23, F24, F29.

**Takeaway:** a strong motif is a portable unit -- but the mechanism that matters (tonal depth, leaf variation) is exactly what copyists drop, so it is the handling, not the motif, that defines the master.

## M9. The tableau of episodes: narrative distributed across the ground

What it IS: in the prints, and by extension the paintings, meaning is not centred in one scene but distributed as small episodes across the picture plane. In Landscape with a Snipe Shooting (1600) the snipe appears four times across the print -- "dead, in danger, taking flight, and soaring safely above the earth. The repetition conveys a narrative sequence"; a fox watches a bird at the base of a dead branch, a half-living half-dead tree stands at the far left, hunters creep in the lower right, wanderers cross a bridge in the middle ground. -- Leg 1, Levesque P4.

**Takeaway:** narrative as a trail of small incidents: scatter the story across the ground plane and the viewer has to walk the picture to collect it.

## M10. Panel and copper: glazing for luminosity in the dark

What it IS: the forest interiors are oil on panel (oak panel; 42 x 61 cm and 56 x 85 cm formats) and the smaller cabinet pieces oil on copper (Cleveland Venus and Adonis 37.8 x 53.6 cm, signed; Prado Landscape 24 x 19 cm, oil on copper). Copper's smooth, non-absorbent surface gives fine detail and a luminous sheen; on panel, multiple layers and glazing build luminosity inside dark passages so light appears to filter through the canopy. -- Leg 1.

**Takeaway:** the support is part of the light system -- smooth copper for crisp miniature detail, glazed panel for depth in darkness.

## M11. Leaf-painting as a generative process (Van Mander's "flourishing movement")

What it IS: Van Mander's Den Grondt treats painting leaves as the test of a painter's power: "It is advisable, in a good picture, to obtain through practice a natural and expert manner of painting leaves; because in that lays your power" -- and the method is to "bring forth leaves by means of a flourishing movement of ink wash on colored paper", trying "many manners -- whether after nature or after pleasing works of art". Leaves, hair and light are "spiritual things" that "can only be conceived and reproduced by the imagination"; colour more than line gives the illusion of life, and the distinction is between description and animation -- light as illumination, not mere reflection. -- Leg 1, Levesque P4.

**Takeaway:** foliage is a movement, not an inventory -- a practised flourishing stroke that grows leaves is the mechanism behind the forest's living quality.

## M12. Workshop scale, pendant pairs, and the market machine

What it IS: Coninxloo ran a large workshop selling internationally (including to the king of Spain), kept a shop in his own house, and produced pendant pairs with matched horizons. His posthumous estate auction (1607) drew nearly the whole Amsterdam art community. The six signed paintings are the secure core of an oeuvre otherwise swamped by attributions and copies. -- Leg 1 F9, F15, F16, F28, F30.

**Takeaway:** the forest landscape was also a product system -- paired panels, prints, workshop copies -- and the "invention" spread because it was reproducible, not because it was unique.

## Works

## W1 -- Landscape with the Judgment of Midas, 1588

Oil on wood (panel); 120 x 204 cm. VERIFIED (Woermann 1887 catalogue window, L7814-7830). Gemaldegalerie Alte Meister, Dresden, inv. 857 (old inv. 791). Signed with the monogram of Coninxloo and dated 1588 per Woermann 1887 and the Ritter thesis (2012/13); 1598 per Boutsen 2018 -- see C639; this study uses 1588. The largest Coninxloo in this list. The figures are probably by Martin van Cleef. Hitherto erroneously ascribed to Lukas Gassel and Hendrick Goltzius. Dense trees stand at the sides of the foreground forming a forest edge -- already the first indications of a woodland character inside a Mannerist world landscape. The composition still carries a full mythological foreground scene; the forest is a frame, not yet the subject. Phase: Antwerp (late), 1588.

## W2 -- Forest Landscape (Forest landscape with a resting hunter), 1598

Oil on panel; 42 x 61 cm. REPORTED (Boutsen 2018; Virtual Interiors caption: "oil on panel, 42 x 61 cm, Vienna: Liechtenstein Collection, Inv. GE751"). Liechtenstein Collection, Vienna, inv. GE751 / inv. 751 (two numbering systems for the same picture; see leg 2 C10). Signed. The work that takes the close-up forest to its extreme: the sky is visible only in a few patches between branches, and a single tiny human figure reclines under a tree. Great intensity and atmospheric quality through fine shades of brown and green and accentuated handling of light. Small format (42 x 61 cm) against the huge Dresden Midas (120 x 204 cm): the forest is now intimate, not monumental. Phase: Amsterdam (early), 1598.

## W3 -- Forest landscape with horsemen, 1604

Oil on panel (medium not confirmed in sources read). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Liechtenstein Collection, Vienna, inv. 753. Signed. One of the six signed paintings (Boutsen 2018). No descriptive text was surfaced for this picture beyond title, date and inventory number. The title indicates horsemen as staffage inside the forest. Phase: Amsterdam, 1604.

## W4 -- Forest landscape with hunters on a wooden bridge, 1605

Medium not confirmed in sources read. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Historisches Museum der Pfalz, Speyer, inv. hm 1975/122. Signed. One of the six signed paintings (Boutsen 2018). No descriptive text surfaced beyond title, date and inventory number. The wooden bridge is a recurring Coninxloo device: a man-made crossing threading the forest interior and giving the eye a route through the trees. Phase: Amsterdam, 1605.

## W5 -- Landscape with Venus and Adonis, 1580s

Dimensions unframed 37.8 x 53.6 cm; framed 48.3 x 64.5 x 7 cm. VERIFIED (Cleveland Museum of Art object page read end to end, 14 lines). Cleveland Museum of Art, acc. 1962.293 (Boutsen gives inv. 62293). Signed "GLVC" lower left. Medium NOT VERIFIED -- the CMA object page as fetched states no medium; do not assert oil on panel. Dated 1580s per Cleveland; undated per Boutsen 2018. Venus fails to dissuade Adonis from hunting the boar that will kill him; the story is nestled in the foreground of an expansive view with Germanic architecture rather than a classical setting. The museum's own text: "Woodlands were relatively new subjects in European painting and this work points toward the full-blown forested landscapes of the 1600s." Provenance note: possibly Gillis van Coninxloo the Elder (inventory of 19 January 1607); sold to Cleveland 1962 via F. Kleinberger, New York. Phase: Antwerp/Frankenthal boundary, 1580s.

## W6 -- Wooded Landscape (Waldlandschaft), c. 1600

Oil on panel; 56 x 85 cm. REPORTED (Boutsen 2018: identical dimensions to the Milotice panel). Kunsthistorisches Museum, Vienna, inv. GG 6504 (inventory number from the Wikimedia Commons file name, not from a KHM page read). Signed: remnants of a monogram, "GvC" with an arrow, on the tree trunk at left (Boutsen). Barely any sky showing through the foliage; the forest looks even denser than the Milotice pendant. No single human figure is portrayed; only small animals can be found. The painting was very popular: it was copied at least six times (Boutsen, citing RKDimages record 262015). One of the six signed paintings. Phase: Amsterdam, c. 1600.

## W7 -- Wooded Landscape, Schloss Milotice, c. 1600

Oil on panel; 56 x 85 cm (written on the back of the RKD photograph). REPORTED. Schloss Milotice, south-east Czech Republic (formerly Korycany castle nearby; in the Milotice collection since 1945). Discovered by Elise Boutsen in the RKD "unmounted material" as a black-and-white photograph. To the right a vista through thick foliage opens onto a castle built on high rocks. Little animals emerge between tree trunks and twisted branches; a monkey can be spotted high in the tree at right. No human figure except one small man running away into the woods. Boutsen: the absence of a narrative is quite exceptional for a Flemish landscape. Trees delicately executed with a good eye for proportion and balance; even in a very dense forest the illusion of depth holds. Seen in person by Boutsen: very rich colours, the typical brownish imprimatura shining through the leaves, thick shrubberies in the foreground in perpetual shadow under gigantic oaks; she could "almost smell the dampness of the mosses". Condition troublesome: severe cracks and flaking of the paint layer. On the back of the panel the name Nicolaes (?) de Groote, in a more recent hand, possibly a former owner. Boutsen argues it is the pendant of W6: identical measurements, the horizon height corresponds, the horizon seems to flow from one painting into the other, and foliage, trees and forest-floor texture are painted the same way. Attribution: Boutsen 2018 (new attribution; not a signed painting). Phase: Amsterdam, c. 1600.

## W8 -- Landscape with a Scene from the Myth of Latona and the Lycian Peasants

Oil on canvas; 143 x 204 cm (143.5 cm height per Wikidata). REPORTED (Wikidata statements; wahooart/topimpressionists agree). State Hermitage Museum, St Petersburg, inv. GE-460. The canvas was sewn from two pieces; the seam is noticeable from the front and passes vertically 81.5 cm from the right edge. Latona (Leto) with her children at a pool; the Lycian peasants who refuse her water. The main attention is on the extremely detailed study of the landscape elements and the complex lighting effects of the thicket. Early history unknown; first recorded 1894 at the Munich sale of the M. Schubert collection, bought for the Hermitage for 3,000 marks. ATTRIBUTION DISPUTED: carried as Coninxloo in Hermitage inventories until 1981; doubts raised 1964 by Willy Laureyssens, who argued it is the joint work of Hendrick van Cleve III (landscape) and Denis van Alsloot; dated c. 1607-1615 by the scholars who reattribute it. Do not use as a secure Coninxloo; do not use it in the evolution chain without the reattribution. Phase: if Coninxloo, Amsterdam; if van Cleve/van Alsloot, after his death.

## W9 -- Landscape (forest landscape), c. 1598

Oil on copper; 24 x 19 cm. REPORTED (artsdot/buypopart only; no Prado object page surfaced). Museo Nacional del Prado, Madrid. A small copper panel: dense thicket in the foreground, leaves rendered with near-obsessive attention to each vein and curl of bark (artsdot description, itself a secondary paraphrase). Copper as a support matters mechanically: a smooth, non-absorbent ground that lets thin oil glazes glow, suited to the small cabinet format. No museum-page confirmation exists in this leg for title, date, medium or dimensions beyond the reproduction sites. Phase: Amsterdam, c. 1598 (if the date holds).

## W10 -- Landscape with Duck Hunters

Date, medium, dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Saarland Museum, Saarbrucken (Wikipedia selected works; repeated by alchetron and hellenicaworld, all copying Wikipedia). Nothing beyond title and collection was found in this leg. The title places hunters and waterfowl in the forest, the same staffage family as W3 and W4. Listed here because Wikipedia names it among the selected works; the parent should not build mechanism claims on it. Phase: undated in sources read.

## W11 -- Woodland with Saint Jerome

Date, medium, dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Museum of Fine Arts, Ghent (MSK), inv. 1991-B (inventory number from the Wikimedia Commons file name). Wikipedia illustrates it in the Gillis II article, but the Wikimedia Commons file is named "Gillis III van Coninxloo - Woodland with Saint Jerome - 1991-B - Museum of Fine Arts Ghent (MSK).jpg" -- i.e. the son (c. 1581-1619/20), who is otherwise known for still lifes. The attribution father-vs-son is unresolved in the sources read. Do not place it in the evolution chain. Phase: unresolved attribution.

## W12 -- Elias is fed by ravens (Elijah fed by the ravens)

Date, medium, dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Royal Museums of Fine Arts of Belgium, Brussels. Nothing beyond title and collection was found in this leg. Elijah by the brook Cherith, ravens bringing bread and meat (1 Kings 17): a hermit-prophet fed in the wilderness, the same "holy figure hidden in the woods" device as W11. The subject also exists as a Roelant Savery workshop painting (Rijksmuseum, 1634, 40 x 49 cm) -- do not confuse the two. Phase: undated in sources read.

## W13 -- Forest Scene (drawing), 1595

Grisaille on black paper (Google Arts & Culture: "by using black paper and showing the forest at night"; "the drawing's large scale and the artist's use of grisaille make it rare"). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. J. Paul Getty Museum, Los Angeles. A haunting night forest, a maze of paths and aged, gnarled trees casting looming shadows. Moonlight glints off twisted roots and leaves, piercing into the groves in the distance, partially illuminating but never fully revealing what is taking place. Humans appear only in the background, framed by twisted trunks: two tiny figures making their way to a moated fortress at the left. The GAC text itself opens "An unknown artist created this haunting scene" -- the attribution to Coninxloo is hedged on the museum's own partner page. Mechanically the important point is the inversion: black paper as the dark ground, light painted in, so the forest is built out of darkness rather than out of light. Rare among the common coloured-wash Dutch/Flemish drawings around 1600. Phase: Frankenthal/Amsterdam boundary, 1595 (if the date holds).

## W14 -- Forest Landscape with Hagar and the Angel (copy after W6), 17th century

Medium and dimensions not stated on the fetched Pinakothek page. Alte Pinakothek, Munich, inv. 6328. A replica of the autograph KHM painting W6. In comparison the foliage is more schematic and less differentiated, and the plant growth is summary (Pinakothek text). The copyist added the Old Testament staffage: Hagar, concubine of Abraham, expelled with her son Ishmael when Sarah bore Isaac; Hagar and Ishmael are saved by an angel who leads them out of the desert. The Pinakothek holds the pendant too (fig. 5 in Boutsen): Hagar and Ishmael being sent away by Abraham, same measurements, a copy after the Milotice panel W7. The pair shows the market mechanism: a successful forest composition was copied, given a biblical subject to sell it, and paired up. Included because the copy is the evidence for W6's popularity and for how the forest formula travelled. Phase: copy, after c. 1600 original.

## W15 -- Forest Landscape (Circle of Coninxloo), between 1600 and 1620

Oil on oak panel; unframed 51.1 x 83.8 cm (20 1/8 x 33 in.). VERIFIED (Detroit Institute of Arts page read end to end, 182 lines). Detroit Institute of Arts, acc. 38.70, Founders Society Purchase, William C. Yawkey Fund (bought 1938; previously Zacharie Birtschansky, Paris). DIA subject tags list donkey, figures, Flanders, forest, hills, path, river, town, trees -- i.e. a wooded road with a path, a river and a town beyond, figures and a donkey on it. Attribution history is the point: published as by Gillis van Coninxloo (Ogden, English Taste in Landscape, 1955) and now catalogued as Circle of Coninxloo (Held, Flemish and German Paintings of the 17th Century, Detroit, 1982). A workshop/circle piece that shows how widely the formula was imitated within a decade of the master's death. Phase: circle, 1600-1620.

## W16 -- Landscape (drawing, follower of Coninxloo), late 16th, early 17th century

Pen and brown ink, with green and blue watercolor, heightened with bodycolor. Dimensions 32.2 x 36 cm (12 11/16 x 14 3/16 in.). VERIFIED (Metropolitan Museum of Art page read end to end, 80 lines). Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York, acc. 80.3.442, Gift of Cornelius Vanderbilt, 1880. Formerly attributed to Paul Bril. In its unfinished state the sheet has an almost ghost-like appearance, partly from the strong contrast of the colours on brown paper. The paper was originally blue and has discoloured through sunlight exposure; in its original state the gouaches would not have stood out so much and the drawing would have been more legible. The anonymous author composed the landscape by assembling visual elements derived from engravings after Coninxloo: a castle on a hilltop with arched structures (as in Coninxloo's Landscape with Samson Killing the Lion) and cottages along a forest edge (as in the Landscape with the Judgement of Paris) -- comparisons by W. Wegner, Oud Holland LXXXII (1967). The draftsman's cautious lines lack the freedom of an original designer; stains of spilled paint in the upper right may be why the sheet was discarded. The Met text states the general mechanism plainly: Coninxloo's detailed, realistic forest interiors were a distinct move away from the fanciful Mannerist mountain valleys of earlier decades, and prints after his designs were an important means of dispersing his inventions. Phase: follower, late 16th/early 17th century.

## W17 -- Wooded landscape with a village (drawing, attributed to Coninxloo), 1559-1607

Pen and brown ink, with brown and blue wash, on paper; height 296 mm, width 395 mm. (From the British Museum search snippet; the BM page itself was NOT read end to end -- chrome only.) British Museum, London, mus. no. 1859,0709.2821. Purchased from A. E. Evans & Sons. Not on display. Bibliographic reference: Popham, Dutch and Flemish drawings of the XV and XVI centuries (1932), no. 1. Trees either side, a wood at right, land sloping down to a valley where there is a village and castle. A drawing in the same language as the paintings: framing trees at the edges, a dip into a settled valley. The production date given is the artist's working life, not a dating of the sheet. Phase: undated within the working life.

## W18 -- A wood; a path disappearing into the centre and a lake at left (drawing, Circle/School of Coninxloo), 1559-1607

Pen and brown ink, with grey-brown and blue wash, on paper; height 184 mm, width 370 mm. (From the British Museum search snippet; page not read end to end.) British Museum, London, mus. no. Gg,2.227. Acquired 1799. Not on display. Curator's comment: similar drawings in the Albertina (cat. 248-250). Bibliographic reference: Popham 1932, no. 3. A path disappearing into the centre of a wood, a lake at the left. The path-into-the-wood is the core Coninxloo routing device in drawing form. Phase: circle/school, undated within the working life.

## W19 -- Wooded Mountain Landscape (drawing, Follower of Coninxloo), mid-1500s to early 1600s

Brush with wash and pen and ink on paper; 23 x 42.5 cm (9 1/16 x 16 3/4 in.). (From the RISD search snippet; the RISD page was not read end to end.) RISD Museum, Providence, acc. 50.303, Gift of Miss Ellen D. Sharpe. Originally attributed to Coninxloo himself; now Follower of Gilles van Coninxloo III. The RISD text is a precise diagnostic of a copy: unlike Coninxloo's own forest interiors, which typically obscure the horizon line, this sheet leaves the horizon visible; the copyist's hand is shown by the unfinished borders, where the lines trail off and lose specificity -- the artist was copying from another source rather than inventing the composition. The dark, tangled forest is not realistically described: the trees are treated as design elements rather than individualized objects. That contrast (obscured horizon + individualized trees = autograph; visible horizon + trees-as-pattern = copy) is a usable attribution test for the whole oeuvre. Phase: follower, mid-1500s-early 1600s.

## W20 -- An elegant party in a river landscape (attributed to Coninxloo II), undated

Oil on panel; 73 x 125.3 cm, framed. (Dorotheum page read end to end, 21 lines; dimensions from the lot text.) Private collection (Seel Collection, Kassel; Kunsthaus Malmede, Cologne before 1931; private Rhenish collection since the 1930s). Sold Dorotheum, Vienna, 20 October 2015, realised EUR 56,250. Registered in the RKD database under no. 73897 as attributed to G. van Coninxloo II; Wilhelm von Bode identified it as a work by Coninxloo. A large-format river landscape with an elegant party -- the "large landscape paintings sold internationally" workshop product of the Wikipedia biography, at a scale between the small Liechtenstein panels and the huge Dresden Midas. The Dorotheum lot text is also the source for the three-phase periodisation used in the Evolution section below (Plietzsch 1910). Phase: attributed; undated.

## Beyond the 20

- **B1.** The Judgment of Midas, engraving by Nicolaes de Bruyn after Gillis II van Coninxloo, 408 x 506 mm (Royal Library of Belgium catalogue record). A print after W1, evidence of how the designs travelled; de Bruyn and Jan van Londerseel were the Flemish emigre engravers who published Coninxloo's designs in the Dutch Republic.
- **B2.** Landscape with Samson Killing the Lion. Known only through W. Wegner, "Zeichnungen von Gillis van Coninxloo und seiner Nachfolge", Oud Holland LXXXII (1967), p. 215, as cited by the Met: it shows a castle on a hilltop with various arched structures surrounding it. No location, date, medium or dimensions found in this leg. Do not assert it as an extant, locatable work.
- **B3.** Landscape with the Judgement of Paris. Known only through Wegner 1967, p. 221, as cited by the Met: it depicts cottages along a forest edge. No location, date, medium or dimensions found. Auction listings (artnet) record a "Waldlandschaft mit dem Paris Urteil" under Coninxloo's name, but no holding collection was verified. Do not assert it as an extant, locatable work.
- **B4.** The Walk to Emmaus (Der Gang nach Emmaus), dated 1588. Recorded by Boutsen as a landscape dated 1588 auctioned at Christie's Monaco, 7 December 1987, lot 12; artnet lists "DER GANG NACH EMMAUS, 1588". Whereabouts unknown; the authenticity of the signature is impossible to determine (Boutsen). If the date holds it is one of the earliest dated Coninxloo landscapes, contemporary with the Midas.
- **B5.** A Forest Landscape with Hunters and Dogs stalking a Stag, oil on panel, 35 x 55 cm, private collection, Belgium (anticstore, ref. 125305). Attributed to Coninxloo II. Catalogue note: "a thick web of trees standing very close to each other. Only scattering light finds its way through the heavy vegetation. The landscape is animated by only a few staffage-figures of hunters and their dogs stalking a stag." Dealer page; treat as attributed, not secure.
- **B6.** Forest Scene with Hunters, Stadel Museum, Frankfurt, oil on panel, 22.1 x 15.4 cm, c. 1600. NOT a Coninxloo work: the Stadel's own digital collection lists it under "Jan Brueghel the Elder; attributed", and FeelTheArt attributes it to Jan Brueghel the Elder. Artera attributes the same picture to Coninxloo; that attribution is wrong. Recorded here only to prevent the parent from using it.
- **B7.** Landscape, pencil, 21 x 31 cm, "Gilles van Coninxloo, 1550-1607", Art Fund record (gift of Alfred Jowett): "View from a wooded road on a hill, overlooking a river. In the distance small dwellings." The Art Fund itself warns the record has not been updated since first publication and may contain inaccurate information. The life dates (1550-1607) are wrong. Do not use without verification.
- **B8.** Copies of the KHM Wooded Landscape (W6), at least six: Muzeum Narodowe, Warsaw, inv. 181831; Daphne Alazraki Fine Arts, New York (2003); sale Amsterdam (P. de Boer), unknown date; sale Vienna (Dorotheum), 21 April 2015, lot 22; sale Amsterdam (Christie's), 14 November 1991, lot 217; second copy auctioned Christie's Amsterdam, 6 May 2008, lot 16 (Boutsen, citing RKDimages 262015). Plus the two Alte Pinakothek copies (W14 and its pendant).

## Evolution

The periodisation is E. Plietzsch's (1910), quoted in the Dorotheum lot text read end to end: three phases marked by the artist's periods in Antwerp (1570-1588), Frankenthal (1588-1595), and Amsterdam (1595-1606). Wikipedia gives the move to Frankenthal as 1587 (he "first resided in Middelburg, Zeeland and then in 1587 he moved to Frankenthal where he was active until 1595"); Plietzsch's phase boundary is 1588. The phase boundary is 1587/1588, not a sharp year. The works in this leg sort into the three phases as follows (attributed and disputed works excluded from the chain).

### Phase 1 -- Antwerp, 1570-1588: the world landscape with trees closing in

Works: W1 Midas (1588, 120 x 204 cm), W5 Venus and Adonis (1580s), B4 Emmaus (1588, if genuine). Composition: Northern Mannerist versions of the established world landscape type: a high viewpoint, a panoramic sweep, a mythological or biblical foreground scene that justifies the picture. But close views of trees already narrow the panoramic view. In the Midas the dense trees at the sides of the foreground form a forest edge -- the wood is a frame around a history scene, not yet the subject. Tree treatment: trees as coulisses (side wings) and as framing masses; the interior of the wood is not yet entered. Figure scale: large. The Midas figures were painted by another hand (probably Martin van Cleef) -- the landscape painter's workshop already worked by collaboration, and the figures are the picture's nominal subject. Venus and Adonis are a full foreground group. Light: even, descriptive Mannerist light; no dappled canopy light yet. Scale of production: a very busy workshop producing large landscape paintings sold internationally, including to the king of Spain. The Midas at 120 x 204 cm is a palace-scale picture; the workshop's output was a commodity as well as an art.

### Phase 2 -- Frankenthal, 1588-1595: the formula is fixed

Works: W13 Forest Scene drawing (1595, if the date holds); W5 Venus and Adonis sits on the boundary. Composition: the Frankenthal formula, as stated in the Bulletin du Musee National de Varsovie 39 (1998) (search excerpt; the article itself was not readable): "from one side a closely viewed foreground with small figures, bordered by a lateral group of high trees, is counterbalanced on the other side of the composition by a distant panorama." Landscape compositions were "constructed of realistically observed elements according to a specific principle which subordinated them to the rules of painterliness and decorativeness" -- observed nature, composed decoratively. Coninxloo became the leading exponent of the "Frankenthal School", alongside Anton Mirou and Pieter Schoubroeck; the 1887 Dresden catalogue notes Schoubroeck was "probably pupil of Gillis van Coninxloo in Frankenthal". Tree treatment: trees are now a lateral mass of high trunks balancing the composition, not just wings. The forest edge is a structural element. Figure scale: small figures in a closely viewed foreground; the figures shrink and the trees grow relative to phase 1. Light: the night-forest drawing W13 (black paper, grisaille, moonlight glinting off twisted roots) shows the Frankenthal-period interest in the forest as a place of mystery and in light as something that pierces the grove rather than flooding it. Historical context: Coninxloo, a Calvinist, left Antwerp after the Spanish reconquest in 1585 and settled in Frankenthal in the Electoral Palatinate two years later. The forest painting of an exile: Martin Papenbrock's thesis, noted in the Ritter abstract, is that the wood in Coninxloo's work carries an "Exilmoment" (exile moment). Recorded as a thesis, not a fact.

### Phase 3 -- Amsterdam, 1595-1606: the apogee; the forest becomes the subject

Works: W2 Forest Landscape 1598, W3 horsemen 1604, W4 bridge 1605, W6 KHM c. 1600, W7 Milotice c. 1600, W9 Prado copper c. 1598. Composition: the close-up forest. Beginning in the 1590s Coninxloo introduced close-up views of forests "reminiscent of Albrecht Altdorfer and the Danube school nearly a century earlier and almost or entirely shutting out a distant view". The Frankenthal formula's "distant panorama" is deleted: the 1598 Liechtenstein Forest Landscape takes the approach to its extreme -- the sky is visible only in a few patches between branches. The Warsaw Bulletin excerpt states the outcome directly: "After 1595 Coninxloo left Frankenthal and settled in Amsterdam, where he experienced the apogee of his creativity." Karel van Mander devoted a chapter of Den Grondt der Edel Vry Schilderconst (1604) to landscape painting and recommended precisely Coninxloo's work as the classic example of the genre. Tree treatment: trees on a hugely exaggerated scale, in elaborate compositions. The RISD follower sheet gives the negative test: in Coninxloo's own forest interiors the horizon line is typically obscured, and the trees are individualized objects, not design elements. The Milotice panel shows the mechanics: delicately executed trees with a good eye for proportion and balance, depth created inside a very dense forest, thick shrubberies in the foreground in perpetual shadow under gigantic oaks, a brownish imprimatura shining through the leaves. The forest is built as a layered screen: foreground thicket in shadow, mid-ground trunks, and a small vista (the castle on high rocks in W7) that opens only at one edge. Figure scale: the human figure collapses. W2 has a single tiny figure reclining under a tree. W6 and W7 have no human figures at all -- only small animals, and in W7 a single small man running away into the woods and a monkey in a tree. The staffage that remains (resting hunter, horsemen, hunters on a bridge) is small, sparse, and incidental. Earlier forest landscapes had used forests as the backdrop for human activity; Coninxloo "turned them into the subject proper by submerging tiny human figures in elaborate compositions of trees". Light: the change is the most mechanical of all. Phase 1 light is even and descriptive. Phase 3 light is filtered and dappled: "fine shades of brown and green and its accentuated handling of light"; "only scattering light finds its way through the heavy vegetation"; moonlight glinting off roots in W13; the brownish imprimatura shining through the leaves as a warm under-glow. Light becomes the thing that moves through the picture -- patches, glints, and a warm ground showing between leaves -- rather than the thing that illuminates it. Format: the late works shrink. The huge palace pictures of the Antwerp phase (120 x 204 cm) give way to cabinet pictures: 42 x 61 cm (W2), 56 x 85 cm (W6/W7), 24 x 19 cm on copper (W9), 37.8 x 53.6 cm (W5). The forest is now something to look into at close range, not a wall to hang a history on. Dissemination: the influence spread in Holland by means of large-scale prints after his designs, engraved by Nicolaes de Bruyn and Jan van Londerseel. The Met follower drawing W16 shows the mechanism at work: a copyist assembling a landscape out of engraved Coninxloo motifs. Van Mander wrote in the Schilder-boeck that he knew "at this time of no better landscape painter, and I notice that they are following his manner very much in Holland".

### What did NOT change

The brown-green palette and the warm ground persist across all three phases; the change is in how much sky and figure the picture admits, not in the colour system. Collaboration and the workshop persist: figures by other hands in phase 1 (Midas), copies and circle works in phase 3 (W14, W15, W19). The oeuvre is small at its secure core -- six signed paintings -- and large at its attributed fringe.

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

Under the definition established in #533 (museum or collection page read end to end): VERIFIED for 5 of the 20 -- W1 Midas (Woermann 1887 catalogue window, 120 x 204 cm), W5 Venus and Adonis (Cleveland, 37.8 x 53.6 cm), W15 Circle panel (Detroit, 51.1 x 83.8 cm), W16 follower drawing (Met, 32.2 x 36 cm), W20 river landscape (Dorotheum lot, 73 x 125.3 cm). REPORTED for 5 -- W2 (Boutsen/Virtual Interiors, 42 x 61 cm), W6 (Boutsen, 56 x 85 cm), W7 (Boutsen/RKD photograph, 56 x 85 cm), W8 (Wikidata, 143 x 204 cm), W9 (reproduction sites, 24 x 19 cm). NOT VERIFIED for 10 -- W3, W4, W10, W11, W12, W13, W14, W17, W18, W19 (W17/W18/W19 dimensions appear only in search snippets of the holding museums' records, not on pages read end to end, and W13/W14 have no dimensions at all in the sources read).

## Aesthetic logic

The first logic is enclosure. The world landscape gave the viewer a high place to stand and a blue distance to travel to; Coninxloo takes both away. The low viewpoint puts the viewer in the undergrowth, the foreground thicket blocks the feet, the trunks block the sides, and the canopy blocks the sky. What is left is a room made of trees, and the picture's feeling is the feeling of being inside a room with no door. The Frankenthal formula kept one lateral opening as a release; the Amsterdam apogee deletes even that, and the sky survives only as patches between branches. A landscape with no path and no horizon is not a view; it is a place.

The second logic is the warm ground. The brownish imprimatura shining through thin greens means the shadow and the earth share one material, and the light that does arrive -- a patch on a reclining man, a glint on a root, a castle on high rocks at the edge -- reads as an event inside the dark rather than as daylight. The forest is dark overall and lit in incidents, which is why the small figures register at all: they are the lit incidents, not the subject. Van Mander understood this as animation rather than description: leaves, hair and light are "spiritual things" that can only be conceived by the imagination, and the flourishing stroke that grows leaves is what makes the trees look alive.

The third logic is the distributed episode. Nothing is centred. The snipe is dead, in danger, taking flight and soaring safely, four times across one print; the fox watches, the hunters creep, the wanderers cross the bridge. The viewer walks the picture the way a traveller walks the landscape, collecting incidents in sequence. That is also why the formula travelled so well in print: the incidents are portable units, and a copyist could assemble a whole landscape out of engraved castles and cottages. What the copyists could not carry was the handling -- the tonal depth, the leaf variation, the warm ground -- which is why the copies are schematic where the originals breathe.

## Positions

- **P1 -- The forest is the subject, not the setting.** The whole move of the oeuvre is enclosure replacing panorama: the trees stop framing a history scene and become the picture. Judge any Coninxloo by how much sky and figure it admits -- the less, the later and the more characteristic.
- **P2 -- Scale is the argument.** Trees on a hugely exaggerated scale, humans as footnotes: the forest reads as a power, not a backdrop. A piece that keeps the figures large has missed the mechanism, whatever trees it paints.
- **P3 -- Light is rationed, not supplied.** Canopy chiaroscuro: fine brown-green gradation with a few accentuated lit patches. The light is the incident; the dark is the substance. Brightness spread evenly across the picture is the Antwerp phase, not the Amsterdam one.
- **P4 -- The forest was also a product system.** Paired panels with matched horizons, large-scale prints after the designs, workshop copies, circle works within a decade of the master's death. The "invention" spread because it was reproducible -- and the copies prove the point by losing exactly the handling that made the originals work.
- **P5 -- He is the elaborator, not the inventor.** Bruegel's 1550s forest drawings and Jan Brueghel's pre-1598 experiments share the priority; Van Mander claims no originality for him. What Coninxloo did was systematise the close-up forest into a formula with a workshop, a print trade and a school behind it. Call him the one who reached the high point, never the sole inventor.

## Quotes

- **Q1 -- Karel van Mander, Schilder-boeck (1604), on Coninxloo:** "I know at this time of no better landscape painter, and I notice that they are following his manner very much in Holland."
- **Q2 -- Wikipedia, citing Vlieghe, on the 1598 Forest Landscape (W2):** the sky is visible only in a few patches between branches, and a single tiny human figure reclines under a tree; it achieves "great intensity and atmospheric quality through its fine shades of brown and green and its accentuated handling of light."
- **Q3 -- Catherine Levesque, on the KHM Forest Landscape:** the viewer looks from a low point past a foreground of "meticulously rendered vegetation, dead branches, and marshy underwood into a substantial woodland" -- and is "provided no access into nor road out of the scene".
- **Q4 -- Elise Boutsen, on the Milotice panel (W7):** the atmospheric forest was rendered in very rich colours "with the typical brownish imprimatura shining through the leaves", and she could "almost smell the dampness of the mosses".
- **Q5 -- Karel van Mander, Den Grondt, on leaf-painting:** "It is advisable, in a good picture, to obtain through practice a natural and expert manner of painting leaves; because in that lays your power."
- **Q6 -- Wikipedia, on the Amsterdam turn:** Coninxloo "turned them into the subject proper by submerging tiny human figures in elaborate compositions of trees."
- **Q7 -- The Metropolitan Museum of Art, on the follower drawing (W16):** Coninxloo's "detailed, realistic forest interiors were a distinct move away from the fanciful Mannerist mountain valleys of earlier decades, and prints after his designs were an important means of dispersing his inventions."

None of these are Coninxloo's own words. No writing by him is known.

## Network

**N1 -- Family: a painting household.** Father Gillis van Coninxloo I (d. c. 1545), painter from Brussels, master in the Antwerp Guild of Saint Luke 1539, died when Gillis was about ten months old. Mother Adriana van Doornicke, daughter of the painter Jan van Dornicke and widow of the painter Jan van Amstel; her sister married Pieter Coecke van Aelst. After her husband's death Adriana made a living selling paintings in Antwerp and in Brabant and Flanders towns, and remarried 20 December 1545 to the painter Peeter van Else, alias van den Winckele. First wife Maria Robroeck, widow of his cousin Pauwels Coecke van Aelst (bastard son of Pieter Coecke van Aelst); Coninxloo abandoned a planned trip to Italy on learning of Pauwels's death and returned to Antwerp to marry her in 1570; at least two children, Catalijne (b. c. 1579) and Gillis III (c. 1581-1619/20), who became a still-life painter and is named among his father's pupils. Second wife Geertgen van Eden, married in Amsterdam 23 August 1603, after Maria's death. (Leg 3 N1-N9.)

**N2 -- Teachers: Coecke the Younger, Kroes, Mostaert, and probably Bol.** Pieter Coecke van Aelst the Younger (d. 1559), son of Coninxloo's uncle Pieter Coecke van Aelst (1502-1550), was his first teacher; after his death in 1559 Coninxloo continued with Lenaert Kroes (no dates found in sources read). After his mother's death in 1562 he lived with Gillis Mostaert the Elder (27 or 28 November 1528 - 28 December 1598), working in his workshop in return for food and lodging, allowed to work for his own account; Mostaert was known for winter landscapes and fire/nocturnal scenes. Hans Bol (16 December 1534 - 20 November 1593) is a probable teacher: Boijmans says "Gillis van Coninxloo, Joris Hoefnagel and David Vinckboons are also considered to have been trained by him". Bol is already carried as a reserve in an old block -- network entry here only. (Leg 3 N10, N13-N15.)

**N3 -- Pupils: Segers above all.** Named pupils: Pieter Brueghel the Younger (1564/65 - 1637/38), Govert Govertsz van Arnhem, Willem van den Bundel, Gillis van Coninxloo III, Jonas van Merle, Hercules Seghers (c. 1589 - c. 1638) and Jacques van der Wijen. Seghers, born Haarlem, was apprenticed to "the leading Flemish landscapist of the day, Gillis van Coninxloo" in Amsterdam in 1606; the apprenticeship was presumably cut short by Coninxloo's death. Seghers and his father bought a number of Coninxloo's works at the auction of the studio contents, as Pieter Lastman did. Seghers became the most experimental Dutch landscapist-etcher (coloured inks, coloured paper and cloth, sugar-bite aquatint); Rembrandt owned eight of his paintings and reworked his plate Tobias and the Angel into the Flight into Egypt. Seghers was the subject of study #537 -- network entry here only. Pieter Brueghel the Younger was also Coninxloo's nephew by marriage, and four paintings by Pieter Bruegel the Elder were recorded in Coninxloo's estate inventory, so the Younger could study his father's prime versions in person in his master's house. (Leg 3 N16-N21.)

**N4 -- The Frankenthal school (1587/88-1595).** Anton Mirou (1578 - 1627?; see leg 3 FLAG -- Sotheby's gives "Antwerp 1570 - 1661 or after"), leading Frankenthaler, worked alongside Coninxloo from c. 1586; his bosky landscapes are "influenced to a great degree by his fellow Frankenthal painter Gillis van Coninxloo". Pieter Schoubroeck, or Schaubroeck (c. 1570 - 10 May 1607), called Coninxloo's pupil; Italy 1595, Nuremberg 1597-1600, Frankenthal 1601 until his death there. Frederik van Valckenborch (1566 Antwerp - 1623 Nuremberg): "His main influence, next to his father, was probably Gillis van Coninxloo". Marten van Valckenborch (1534 - 1612; see leg 3 FLAG on the birth year) and his son Gillis van Valckenborch (1569 - 1622; see FLAG) are the family frame. Hendrik van der Borcht the Elder (1583 - 26 July 1651; see FLAG -- the Met gives 1660), pupil of Gillis van Valckenborch, recorded in Frankenthal 1611-1627, "considered a member of the Frankenthal school in his landscape work"; his son Hendrik the Younger (1614 - 1665/1690) later kept the Earl of Arundel's collection. Frederick III, Elector Palatine (1515-1576), the Calvinist protector under whom the Flemish Protestant refugees settled in Frankenthal. (Leg 3 N22-N29.)

**N5 -- The influence line: the forest after Coninxloo.** Jan Brueghel the Elder (1568 - 13 January 1625): Coninxloo "exercised a strong influence on Jan Brueghel the Elder" -- but Brueghel "experimented with such works before Coninxloo's first dated wooded landscape of 1598" and was "along with artists such as Gillis van Coninxloo one of the prime developers of the dense forest landscape" -- the priority is shared, not Coninxloo-alone. Paul Bril (1554 Antwerp - 1626 Rome), the Antwerp landscapist in Rome from the late 16th century; befriended Jan Brueghel the Elder in Rome 1592-94 and collaborated with him and Rottenhammer; the Borghese Fantastic Landscape notes "references to the Flemish painters of the Frankenthal school and the work of Gillis van Coninxloo, and in particular, Frederik van Valckenborch". Roelant Savery (1576 Kortrijk - buried 25 February 1639): "Savery primarily painted landscapes in the Flemish tradition of Gillis van Coninxloo, often embellished with many meticulously painted animals and plants"; court painter to Emperors Rudolf II and Mathias; Tyrol plant studies 1606-08; the dodo's most prolific illustrator. David Vinckboons (baptised 13 August 1576 Mechelen - c. 1632 Amsterdam): his early wooded landscapes are "most indebted to the Frankenthal painter Gillis van Coninxloo"; with Bol and Savery he developed genre painting in the north. Alexander Keirincx (1600 - 1652): early works "greatly indebted to his older colleagues such as Abraham Govaerts (who may have been his master) and Gillis van Coninxloo (1544-1606)". Gillis d'Hondecoeter (c. 1575/80 - 1638), pupil of Savery and of Vinckboons; father of Melchior. Abraham Govaerts (1589 - 9 September 1626), already carried as a #552 spare -- network entry here only. Gijsbrecht Leytens (1586 - after 1643 and before 1656), the "Master of the Winter Landscapes", "influenced by Jan Brueghel the Elder and Gillis van Coninxloo". Joos de Momper the Younger (1564 - 1635), already carried as a #552 alternate; Lodewijk Toeput, called il Pozzoserrato (c. 1540/1550 - between 1603 and 1605), already carried as a #552 alternate -- network entries here only. (Leg 3 N30-N42.)

**N6 -- Printmakers and publishers after him.** Nicolaes de Bruyn (1571 Antwerp - 1656 Rotterdam): "known primarily among print specialists for his large engraved landscapes after designs by artists such as Gillis van Coninxloo and David Vinckboons from around 1600"; c. 400 prints over a sixty-year career; trained by his uncle Abraham de Bruyn (c. 1539 Antwerp - 1587 Cologne?; see FLAG). Jan van Londerseel (c. 1570/72 - c. 1625), de Bruyn's brother-in-law and only follower/pupil, engraved after Coninxloo's designs for the Dutch market. Assuerus van Londerseel (1572 - 1635) published de Bruyn's plates. Matthaus Merian the Elder (22 September 1593 Basel - 19 June 1650) engraved 26 plates after Anton Mirou's Schwalbach drawings, published 1620. Andries Jacobsz. Stock (c. 1580 Antwerp - c. 1648 The Hague), pupil of Jacques de Gheyn II, engraved the portrait of Coninxloo published by Hendrik Hondius I (1573 Duffel - 1650 Amsterdam) in the 1610 Pictorum aliquot celebrium portrait series. Jacques de Gheyn II (1565 - 29 March 1629) is already carried as a #553 main -- network entry here only. (Leg 3 N43-N50.)

**N7 -- Patrons, biographers, collectors.** Philips of Marnix, Lord of Saint-Aldegonde (1540 - 15 December 1598; see FLAG on the birth year), outer mayor of Antwerp during the Siege; Coninxloo received "in January 1585 a large monetary reward from the outer mayor of Antwerp Philips of Marnix for his role in the defence", then left as the city fell. The King of Spain (Philip II, reigned 1556-1598): Coninxloo's workshop "produced large landscape paintings which were sold internationally including to the king of Spain". Karel van Mander (1548 - 1606), whose Schilder-Boeck (1604) is the principal early source on Coninxloo; already carried as an extra spare -- network entry here only. Pieter Lastman (1583 - 1633) bought Coninxloo's works at the studio-contents auction; Rembrandt's later teacher. Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627 - 1678), whose Inleyding (1678) boosted Seghers's posthumous reputation; already carried as a spare -- network entry here only. Jan van de Cappelle (bapt. 25 January 1626 - 1679), collector of five Seghers paintings; already carried as a #538 reserve -- network entry here only. Johannes Ruisscher (c. 1625 - after 1675), the etcher who closely imitated Seghers. Frederick Henry, Prince of Orange (1584 - 1647), who "bought landscapes in 1632" from Seghers. Rudolf II, Holy Roman Emperor (1552 - 1612), whose Prague court employed Savery; patron only, not proposed. (Leg 3 N51-N59.)

**N8 -- Precursors: the forest before Coninxloo.** Albrecht Altdorfer (c. 1480 - 1538): Coninxloo's close-up forests are "reminiscent of Albrecht Altdorfer and the Danube school nearly a century earlier". Joachim Patinir (c. 1480 - 1524): the panoramic "world landscape" whose manner the wooded landscape broke from. Pieter Bruegel the Elder (c. 1525/30 - 1569): "we now know that this kind of landscape had been developed earlier on, as can be seen in some drawings by Pieter Bruegel the Elder (1526/30-1569) from the 1550s" -- the "inventor of the wooded landscape" claim for Coninxloo is now qualified. His son Pieter the Younger was Coninxloo's pupil (N3). (Leg 3 N61-N63.)

**N9 -- The print-mediated link back to #553 (Goudt).** Hendrick Goudt (subject of #553; c. 1583 - 17 December 1648). The doorway link: Goudt engraved Elsheimer's wooded nights and carried them north in print; Seghers (N3) made a free copy of Goudt's print of Elsheimer's lost Large Tobias -- Seghers's Tobias and the Angel plate is "in fact a free copy by Seghers of the Goudt print of Adam Elsheimer's 'Large Tobias' painting, now lost" -- and Rembrandt then reworked that Seghers plate. So the chain runs Elsheimer to Goudt (print) to Seghers (copy) to Rembrandt (rework), with Coninxloo as Seghers's teacher at the head of it. Aegidius Sadeler II (1570 - 1629), already carried as a #553 main, is the Prague print line: imperial engraver at Rudolf II's court, the same court that employed Savery. (Leg 3 N64-N65.)

**N10 -- Held names.** Jacob Savery (c. 1565 - 1603), Roelant's older brother and teacher, Hans Bol's best pupil (van Mander) -- proposed as a spare below, not held. Claes Jansz. Visscher (1587 - 1652) and Esaias van de Velde (c. 1587 - 1630), Vinckboons pupils, already carried -- network entries here only. Hans (Jan) Savery II (1589 - buried 7 August 1654), Roelant's nephew, already carried as a #553 spare -- network entry here only. The four thin pupils (van Arnhem, van den Bundel, van Merle, van der Wijen) have no dates in any source read this run. Lenaert Kroes (N2) likewise has no dates. Rembrandt and Vermeer are NOT proposed, per the standing hold.

## Future candidates

Top pick for #555 is Roelant Savery (1576 - buried 25 February 1639), the forest tradition's heir: Coninxloo's pupil Segers etched the night into print, and Savery took Coninxloo's forest to Rudolf II's Prague court and filled it with animals, plants and dodos. Hop from the Coninxloo study: the forest that left Antwerp for Prague -- Savery painted landscapes in the Flemish tradition of Coninxloo, embellished with meticulously painted animals and plants, at the court whose zoo gave him the dodo; the doorway is the forest that became a natural-history cabinet. New from #554: Mains: Roelant Savery (1576 - buried 25 February 1639) -- forest tradition's heir, Prague court painter to Rudolf II and Mathias, Tyrol plant studies 1606-08, the dodo's most prolific illustrator; Paul Bril (1554 Antwerp - 1626 Rome) -- the Antwerp landscapist who carried the Flemish forest to Rome, befriended Jan Brueghel the Elder there 1592-94, collaborated with him and Rottenhammer, and his work carries Frankenthal/Coninxloo references; Jan Brueghel the Elder (1568 - 13 January 1625) -- Coninxloo's co-developer of the dense forest landscape, who experimented with wooded glades before Coninxloo's first dated wooded landscape of 1598, friend and collaborator of Paul Bril and Peter Paul Rubens, inventor of paradise landscapes and flower garlands. Reserve: David Vinckboons (baptised 13 August 1576 Mechelen - c. 1632 Amsterdam) -- the Amsterdam line, family fled Antwerp, settled Amsterdam 1591, his early wooded landscapes "most indebted to the Frankenthal painter Gillis van Coninxloo", with Bol and Savery he developed genre painting in the north, teacher of Gillis d'Hondecoeter and Claes Jansz. Visscher. Alternates: Anton Mirou (1578 - 1627?; see FLAG) -- leading Frankenthaler, worked alongside Coninxloo from c. 1586, his bosky landscapes "influenced to a great degree by his fellow Frankenthal painter Gillis van Coninxloo", his 1615 Schwalbach drawings engraved by Merian in 26 plates (1620); Pieter Schoubroeck, or Schaubroeck (c. 1570 - 10 May 1607) -- Frankenthal school member, called Coninxloo's pupil, Italy 1595, Nuremberg 1597-1600, Frankenthal 1601 until his death there. Spares: Frederik van Valckenborch (1566 Antwerp - 1623 Nuremberg) -- imaginary Mannerist landscapes, "his main influence, next to his father, was probably Gillis van Coninxloo"; Alexander Keirincx (1600 - 1652) -- Antwerp-trained, early works "greatly indebted to ... Gillis van Coninxloo", emigrated north 1626-28, worked for Charles I in London 1640/41, settled Amsterdam 1643; Gillis d'Hondecoeter (c. 1575/80 - 1638) -- pupil of Roelant Savery and of David Vinckboons, the bridge from Coninxloo's forest into the Dutch animal landscape, father of Melchior; Nicolaes de Bruyn (1571 Antwerp - 1656 Rotterdam) -- the engraver who carried Coninxloo's designs into the Dutch print market, large engraved landscapes after Coninxloo and Vinckboons from c. 1600, c. 400 prints over sixty years; Jan van Londerseel (c. 1570/72 - c. 1625) -- de Bruyn's brother-in-law, only follower and pupil, publisher and engraver of the Coninxloo-derived large landscapes; Gijsbrecht Leytens (1586 - after 1643 and before 1656) -- the "Master of the Winter Landscapes", "influenced by Jan Brueghel the Elder and Gillis van Coninxloo", wooded landscapes with small figures bracketed by repoussoir trees; Gillis van Coninxloo III (c. 1581 - 1619/20) -- the son and pupil, known for still lifes rather than his father's forests, the family line in one generation; Jacob Savery (c. 1565 - 1603) -- Roelant's older brother and teacher, Hans Bol's best pupil (van Mander), the bench that made the Prague dodo-painter. Philipp Uffenbach (15 January 1566 - 6 April 1636; dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3) -- main 2: the Frankfurt teacher who taught Elsheimer before Rome -- sundials, geometry, mechanics and alchemy in the German workshop tradition. Frankfurt painter, etcher and printmaker; trained by Hans Grimmer; published the Zeitweiser sundial booklet in 1598. The doorway is the German workshop: Uffenbach is the bench Elsheimer sat on before Italy, the sundial-maker who taught a painter to measure light before he ever painted it. Johann (Hans) Rottenhammer (1564 - 14 August 1625; dates search-verified only) -- main 3: the Venetian cabinet-painting master who employed Elsheimer as an assistant in 1598/99 and introduced him to Bril -- the first German artist to specialise in cabinet paintings on copper, the format Elsheimer made his own. Rottenhammer collaborated with Bril, Jan Brueghel the Elder and Elsheimer, and two of his drawings belonged to Elsheimer as gifts. The doorway is the format itself: Rottenhammer invented the German cabinet copper in Venice, and Elsheimer took it to Rome and made it the nocturne. David Teniers the Elder (1582 - 29 July 1649; dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3) -- main 4: the Antwerp painter who studied under Elsheimer in Rome and lodged with him -- the one documented case of Elsheimer teaching a painter in person, and the four Vienna landscapes painted under Elsheimer's influence. He studied under Rubens in Antwerp, and subsequently under Elsheimer in Rome. The doorway is the only pupil: Teniers is the one painter Elsheimer actually taught, face to face, in the Rome lodging -- the control case against the print-mediated Dutch line. Matthijs Bril (1550 - 1584 per the 1822 James catalogue; dates search-verified only) -- Paul Bril's brother; arrived in Rome c. 1575; collaborated with Rottenhammer; Claude Wikipedia names him in the Bril circle. FLAG: death 1584 (James 1822) vs commonly given 1583.

Cuyp (December 1612 - 28 August 1652), Jan Wynants (c. 1631/32 - buried 23 January 1684), Philips Wouwerman (bapt. 24 May 1619 - 19 May 1668), Jan Hackaert (bapt. 1 February 1628 - 1685).

Aelbert Cuyp (20 October 1620 - 15 November 1691; dates search-verified only) -- main 1: the friend who painted the daylight into Aert's moonlight. Shared canvases: in the Louvre Landscape at Sunset Cuyp "evidently painted the foreground and cows" (Britannica); in a National Gallery picture he signs his name on the pail of a milkmaid whose figure and red skirt he painted; in the Staedel picture the fishermen, dog and sportsman "are Cuyp's". The golden light against Aert's silver one -- the daylight pair to the nocturne, and the study the series needs next. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; 3 whole-log mentions (5002, 5009, 5127), all carries; no local file; muse slug study/aelbert-cuyp.md 404, re-checked by curl this run. Promoted out of the carried #534 block, which now carries 9 names.

Jan van Goyen (13 January 1596 - 27 April 1656; dates search-verified only) -- main 2: the tonal root Aert's moonlight inverts. Van Goyen, Salomon van Ruysdael and Pieter de Molijn founded the tonal landscape of the 1630s -- thin paint, low horizon, a near-monochrome palette -- and Aert's coloured-light nocturne is the same thin paint turned to night. The bankruptcy parallel is exact: van Goyen died 18,000 guilders in debt after speculating in tulips and property; Aert went bankrupt in 1662 and died in poverty. The two failures frame the same market. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; 1 whole-log mention (5009), a carry; no local file; muse slug study/jan-van-goyen.md 404, re-checked by curl this run. Jacob van Ruisdael (c. 1628/29 - 10 March 1682; dates search-verified only) -- reserve: the pre-eminent Dutch landscape painter, and the negative case. The Stadel says he painted nearly every landscape subject "with the important exception of the Italian landscape and nocturnal landscape" -- the one great Dutch landscapist who did NOT paint the night. A study of Ruisdael after Aert defines the nocturne by its absence. Hendrick Avercamp (bapt. 27 January 1585 - buried 15 May 1634; dates search-verified only) -- alternate 1: "De Stomme van Kampen", the first Dutch painter to specialise in winter scenes, and the root of Aert's second subject. Aert's winter pictures are Avercamp's encyclopaedic formula re-lit: "he returned instead to the broader views and compositional motifs that Avercamp had made famous earlier in the century" (Sotheby's). Jacob van Loo (1614 - 26 November 1670; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 119 lines) -- main 1: Eglon's teacher, and the only pupil Van Loo ever had in Amsterdam was Eglon (Leiden); the conversational grouping as a system -- mythological and biblical scenes staged as lively dialogue, female nudes his contemporaries rated above Rembrandt's -- and the flight: a fatal stabbing in an inn in 1660, death sentence in absentia, and a second career in Paris ending in the Academie royale. The study would be the teacher whose influence his pupil's work does not show -- the honest negative case. The doorway is the teacher whose pupil shows no trace of him.
